=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two strings S and T consisting of lowercase English letters. Here, S and T have equal lengths.
Let X be an empty array, and repeat the following operation until S equals T:

- Change one character in S, and append S to the end of X.

Find the array of strings X with the minimum number of elements obtained in this way. If there are multiple such arrays with the minimum number of elements, find the lexicographically smallest one among them.
 What is lexicographical order on arrays of strings?
A string S = S_1 S_2 \ldots S_N of length N is lexicographically smaller than a string T = T_1 T_2 \ldots T_N of length N if there exists an integer 1 \leq i \leq N such that both of the following are satisfied:

-  S_1 S_2 \ldots S_{i-1} = T_1 T_2 \ldots T_{i-1}
-  S_i comes earlier than T_i in alphabetical order.

An array of strings X = (X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_M) with M elements is lexicographically smaller than an array of strings Y = (Y_1,Y_2,\ldots,Y_M) with M elements if there exists an integer 1 \leq j \leq M such that both of the following are satisfied:

-  (X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_{j-1}) = (Y_1,Y_2,\ldots,Y_{j-1})
-  X_j is lexicographically smaller than Y_j.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
S
T

Output

Let M be the number of elements in the desired array. Print M + 1 lines.
The first line should contain the value of M.
The i + 1-th line (1 \leq i \leq M) should contain the i-th element of the array.

Constraints


- S and T are strings consisting of lowercase English letters with length between 1 and 100, inclusive.
- The lengths of S and T are equal.

Sample Input 1

adbe
bcbc

Sample Output 1

3
acbe
acbc
bcbc

Initially, S = adbe.
We can obtain X = ( acbe , acbc , bcbc ) by performing the following operations:

- 
Change S to acbe and append acbe to the end of X.

- 
Change S to acbc and append acbc to the end of X.

- 
Change S to bcbc and append bcbc to the end of X.

Sample Input 2

abcde
abcde

Sample Output 2

0

Sample Input 3

afwgebrw
oarbrenq

Sample Output 3

8
aawgebrw
aargebrw
aarbebrw
aarbebnw
aarbebnq
aarbeenq
aarbrenq
oarbrenq

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Implementation:
import sys
S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
T = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
cur = list(S)
X=[]
while cur != list(T): # or while any
    best_idx = -1
    best_candidate = None
    for i, (c, t) in enumerate(zip(cur,T)):
        if c != t:
            cand = cur.copy()
            cand[i] = t
            if best_candidate is None or cand < best_candidate:
                best_candidate = cand
                best_idx = i
    cur[best_idx] = T[best_idx]
    X.append(''.join(cur))
print(len(X))
for s in X: print(s)

This candidate will be smaller than any candidate at later position? Let's examine. If there is any mismatched position i with T[i] < S[i], then changing i yields a string with at earliest differing position i smaller than current. Any candidate at j>i has at i current[i]=S[i] (because j>i doesn't change i), so candidate_i is smaller. What about candidate at j<i? If j<i, earliest difference j; if T[j] < S[j], candidate_j smaller; if T[j] > S[j], candidate_i? At j, candidate_i has S[j], candidate_j has T[j]. If T[j] > S[j], then candidate_i is smaller than candidate_j. So the lex smallest candidate is not simply leftmost improving? Let's analyze. We can choose any mismatched position. Candidate_i compared to current: at i, T[i] vs S[i]. If T[i] < S[i], candidate_i is lexicographically smaller than current. If T[i] > S[i], candidate_i is lexicographically larger than current. But among candidates, the one with smallest string may be one where T[i] < S[i] at earliest possible position? Let's derive: For candidate_i, the first position where it differs from current is i. To compare candidate_i and candidate_j with i<j, at position i candidate_i has T[i], candidate_j has S[i]. So if T[i] < S[i], candidate_i is smaller; if T[i] > S[i], candidate_j is smaller. Thus among two candidates, the one at the earlier position i is smaller iff changing i decreases current at i (T[i] < S[i]); otherwise the later candidate is smaller. This means the lex smallest candidate is the leftmost position i such that T[i] < S[i]? Wait if there is an earlier position i with T[i] < S[i], then candidate_i beats all later candidates. If there is an earlier position i with T[i] > S[i], then candidate_i loses to all later candidates, so we skip it. If T[i] == S[i] (not mismatched), no candidate. Thus scan from left to right: first mismatched position with T[i] < S[i] gives best candidate? What about if no such position (all mismatches have T[i] > S[i])? Then for any i<j, candidate_i loses to later candidate because at i T[i] > S[i]. So the best candidate is the rightmost mismatched position? Let's test. Example S='az', T='za': mismatches pos0 a->z (T>S), pos1 z->a (T<S). Leftmost T<S is pos1, candidate pos1 "aa" best. Yes. Example S='ba', T='cb': mismatches pos0 b->c (T>S), pos1 a->b (T>S). No T<S. Rightmost mismatched pos1 candidate "bb"; pos0 candidate "ca". "bb" smaller. Yes. Example S='ca', T='db': pos0 c->d (T>S), pos1 a->b (T>S). Rightmost pos1 "cb" vs pos0 "db"; "cb" smaller. Example S='za', T='ab': pos0 z->a (T<S), pos1 a->b (T>S). Leftmost T<S pos0 candidate "aa"; pos1 "zb"; "aa" smaller. So indeed lex smallest candidate can be found by scanning left to right for first mismatch where T[i] < S[i]; if none, choose rightmost mismatch. But our O(d*N) candidate compare is simpler and less error-prone. However, maybe this insight can produce even simpler algorithm? Let's think: Greedy step with candidate compare can be optimized but not needed. But maybe there is a hidden issue: If we choose candidate by full comparison, we don't need this. N=100, fine.

But wait, is the greedy step's candidate set all positions where cur[i] != target[i]. At later steps, cur[i] may be T[i] for processed positions, and for unprocessed positions cur[i] = S[i]. So condition T[i] < S[i] still holds for unprocessed. The scan rule would choose first unprocessed position with T[i] < S[i] (where target char alphabetically smaller than original), else rightmost unprocessed. Is that equivalent? This scan rule could be used to avoid candidate list. But is it always correct? Let's prove: At current state, for each unprocessed position i, candidate_i differs from current at i. For i<j, candidate_i is lex smaller than candidate_j iff T[i] < current[i] (since at i candidate_i has T[i], candidate_j has current[i]). If T[i] > current[i], candidate_j is smaller. Thus:
- If there exists unprocessed i with T[i] < current[i], let i be the smallest such index. For any j>i, candidate_i beats candidate_j. For any j<i, since i is smallest with T<current, at j either T[j] > current[j] (mismatch) (or no candidate). Then candidate_j loses to candidate_i? Wait compare candidate_j vs candidate_i with j<i. At position j, candidate_j has T[j], candidate_i has current[j]. Since T[j] > current[j], candidate_i is smaller than candidate_j. Thus candidate_i beats all earlier candidates too. So best is first (leftmost) T<current.
- If no such index, then for every unprocessed i, T[i] > current[i]. For any i<j, candidate_i loses to candidate_j (at i T[i] > current[i]). Thus the best is the rightmost unprocessed index.
This is a neat O(d*N) without list copying. But full candidate compare is simpler to reason and less risk? Both fine. The scan rule is also simple and might be more efficient. But need be absolutely sure it applies when current[i] for unprocessed is S[i], and processed positions current[i]=T[i] not considered. For processed positions, T[i]=current[i], no candidate. The condition T[i] < current[i] only for unprocessed. Good.

# candidate compare:
cur = list(S); target=list(T); ans=[]
while cur != target:
    best = None
    for i in range(n):
        if cur[i] != target[i]:
            cand = cur.copy()
            cand[i] = target[i]
            if best is None or cand < best:
                best = cand
                best_i = i
    cur[best_i] = target[best_i]
    ans.append(''.join(cur))

Now, final code should include sys.stdin.read().split() to handle input lines. Use:
import sys
def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    S = data[0].strip()
    T = data[1].strip()
    cur = list(S)
    target = list(T)
    ans = []
    while cur != target:
        best = None
        best_i = -1
        for i in range(len(cur)):
            if cur[i] != target[i]:
                cand = cur[:]
                cand[i] = target[i]
                if best is None or cand < best:
                    best = cand
                    best_i = i
        cur[best_i] = target[best_i]
        ans.append(''.join(cur))
    print(len(ans))
    for s in ans:
        print(s)
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

while cur != target:
    chosen = -1

        if cur[i] != target[i] and target[i] < cur[i]:
            chosen = i
            break
    if chosen == -1:
        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):

                chosen = i
                break

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Output M + 1 lines" if M=0, print 0 and no extra newline? print adds
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    S = data[0]
    T = data[1]

    cur = list(S)
    target = list(T)
    ans = []

    while cur != target:
        best = None
        best_i = -1

        for i in range(len(cur)):
            if cur[i] != target[i]:
                cand = cur[:]
                cand[i] = target[i]

                if best is None or cand < best:
                    best = cand
                    best_i = i

        cur[best_i] = target[best_i]
        ans.append(''.join(cur))

    print(len(ans))
    for s in ans:
        print(s)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
